| 2026-10-20 | PD-13 command hardened by impl-manager (TASK-001): the lock files are generated with `uv pip compile --universal --generate-hashes --only-binary :all: --emit-build-options` (plus `--system-certs` on this machine, because the network intercepts TLS; verification stays on). Reason: without `--only-binary :all:` the lock listed sdist hashes, so with one wheel hash altered pip fell back to the matching sdist and built it in an isolated environment that downloads the build backend WITHOUT hash checks, a hole in TS §8.1 "hashed Python dependencies". The Linux `pip install` of the locks is NOT RUN locally (a `uv --python-platform` proxy passes for x86_64 and aarch64); CI proves it. | impl-manager finding, confirmed by lead against the report; strengthens the approved control. |
| 2026-10-20 | M6 carry-over from the TASK-004 review: `x/sys/unix` and `net.FileListener` are forbidden in non-test agent code now (with `syscall.Socket`/`Bind`); the runner and capread need systemd socket activation, so M6 adds narrowly scoped, two-approval exceptions for those packages only. Residual risks the lint cannot close (go:linkname, fcgi.Serve, other packages) stay with code review. Agent lint must run on a Linux runner in CI because `_linux.go` files are not linted on the Windows dev box. `//nolint` in non-test agent files is blocked by a TASK-010 CI grep step. | Reviewer findings on TASK-004; decided by lead within REQ-012 and TS §2.2. |

| 2026-10-20 | Final lock command (TASK-001 revision 2): `uv pip compile --system-certs --universal --only-binary :all: --emit-build-options --generate-hashes --exclude-newer 2026-10-19T16:56:37Z --python-version 3.12 [--extra dev] pyproject.toml -o <lock>`. Only header line 2 of each lock changed; every pin and hash is identical, and a rerun reproduces the locks byte for byte. `--system-certs` is needed only behind this machine's TLS interception and can be dropped elsewhere. Moving the cutoff forward is a deliberate, reviewed dependency update. | impl-manager revision 2; tester CHK-M0-012 follow-up. |

| 2026-10-20 | Container runtime set up (user choice: WSL2 + Docker Engine inside it). WSL2 and Ubuntu 24.04 were installed with `wsl --install` (one UAC prompt); Docker Engine 29.9.1 from Docker's apt repository runs under systemd inside the distro; Python 3.12 was added there. The corporate TLS-inspection root ("Zscaler Root CA", read from the Windows trust store, SHA-1 0D:23:EE:8F:...:F0:C5) was added to the Ubuntu trust store only, so TLS verification stays on; nothing on Windows was changed. Docker is NOT available from Windows (no docker CLI and no exposed TCP socket, on purpose: an unauthenticated Docker socket would give local root). The PostgreSQL tests therefore run inside WSL (`CI=true`, project copied to `/root/bitscan`, hashed dev lock installed there); a Windows `pytest` run still skips them with `not run: Docker unavailable`. Result: 58 passed, 0 skipped on Linux; the pinned postgres digest and the Ryuk `tag@digest` form both pull; no container is left behind. | User request "fix the docker runtime and make sure all the pytests pass". |
| 2026-10-20 | `systemd-analyze verify` (G17) was run for the first time, in WSL2 Ubuntu 24.04 (systemd 255.4-1ubuntu8.17), reproducing `units.yml`: all nine units pass with no output and exit 0; four planted defects go red. This is not a spec question: no warning was printed for the units as written. One environment note for the first CI run and for any local run from Windows: units must have mode 0644 (git records `100644`); on a Windows drive mounted in WSL they appear as 0777 and systemd prints "marked executable / world-writable" warnings, which the zero-output rule in `units.yml` would treat as a failure. Do not filter those warnings; run the check from a Linux filesystem copy, as CI does. The three Linux stubs also ran correctly. | Lead run after the user's go-ahead; closes G17 and CHK-M0-146 locally. |
| 2026-10-20 | **Milestone reorder (user-approved plan; supersedes the M1-M8 order of the earlier plan).** Goal: agent enrolment, heartbeat and `os_info` + dpkg inventory first; compliance checks later. M0 is unchanged and nothing from it is deleted. M1 must stay first because inventory jobs live in `scan_job`, which references `scan` and `policy`, and the first migration seeds `system_schedule` and creates the `scan_result` partitions (TS §5.2 L637-L1014, L1046), so the whole DDL is needed. New order: **M1** DB, config loader, `db migrate`, Debian version compare, audit chain core, pytester test for the conftest hook, harness on the app roles. **M2** auth core and API plumbing (old M3). **M3** agent API and scheduler, inventory only, plus enrolment tokens/requests, approve/reject, `GET /agents*`, frozen contract schemas incl. Job and Results (old M4 + part of M5). **M4** Go agent and `.deb`, pre-release 0.x (old M6 part). **M5** minimal host UI and package search (old M7 part + M5 part). **M6** first-release gate: enrol, approve, heartbeat, inventory on 3 VMs, heartbeat load test, inventory alerts (old M8 part). **M7** compliance catalog: importer, validator, BIT102, store, rebuild, delete (old M2). **M8** evaluator (old M2). **M9** scan engine on the manager (old M4 + M5 parts). **M10** agent runner, sandbox, redaction, capread, executor, spool (old M6 part). **M11** results, re-evaluation, results UI, retention, nightly end-to-end, N / N-1 compatibility (old M5, M7, M8 parts). Two approvals stay for auth (M2), enrolment and agent routes (M3), Go agent (M4), importer (M7) and runner (M10). | User request "deliver the agent heartbeat and os_info first, compliance later"; three read-only analyses against the spec. |

Old M-numbers in existing reports, reviews and plans are history and are not rewritten; this table is the mapping. | Consequence of the reorder. |

No extra REVOKE (it would be a grant change the spec does not list). The schema-diff test excludes it, and M2's `/readyz` reads it for "migrations current". Known side effect: the app role could INSERT a row into it; if the owner later wants that closed, it is a new grant decision. **Question #40 answered by the user:** the schema-diff test extracts the TS §5.2 SQL blocks from the spec file at test time and applies them as `bitscan_owner` to a separate scratch database in the same container, used only as the reference; it compares catalog queries of both (tables, columns, types, defaults, CHECKs, indexes, grants, trigger, view), excluding the monthly partitions and `alembic_version`. | 2026-10-20 | **Questions #42, #43 and #44 answered by the user (all as proposed).** #42: seeded `setting.value` is a bare JSON number for the five numeric keys (`365`, `300`, `7`, `2`, `50`) and a JSON array of the five default redaction-pattern strings, in spec order, for `redaction_patterns`; each string decodes to the spec line byte for byte (TS §8.6 L1329-L1339). #43: monthly `scan_result` partitions use UTC month boundaries (`FROM ('2026-10-01 00:00:00+00') TO ('2026-11-01 00:00:00+00')`), named `scan_result_YYYY_MM`; `db maintain` finds existing partitions by their bounds, not by name. #44: `db maintain` drops a partition when its upper bound is at least 400 days before now (UTC), whether or not the `scan` rows are gone yet (the retention task deletes them by its own rule); `db maintain` refuses to create a partition over rows sitting in the default partition; the default-partition alert is raised by the manager's alert code (M2 or later), not by `db maintain`. | User answers on 2026-10-20. |
| 2026-10-20 | **Questions #38 and #39 answered by the user (audit chain), as proposed.** #38: `row_hash` is SHA-256, lowercase hex, over the UTF-8 bytes of the compact JSON array `[prev_hash, audit_id, actor, action, target, detail_text, at_text]`, computed in Python; `detail_text` is PostgreSQL's own `detail::text` (or JSON null), `at_text` is `YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM:SS.ffffffZ` in UTC, the first entry's `prev_hash` is 64 × `0`; compact means separators `,` and `:` with no spaces and non-ASCII kept as UTF-8 (`ensure_ascii=False`). #39: an `audit.checkpoint` entry has `action = 'audit.checkpoint'` and `detail = {"last_deleted_id": <int>, "last_deleted_row_hash": "<hex>"}`; the oldest remaining entry is valid if its `prev_hash` is the genesis value, or equals `last_deleted_row_hash` of a checkpoint whose `last_deleted_id` is lower than that entry's id; the checkpoint is itself an ordinary chained entry. The retention task that writes checkpoints is M11; M1 builds append and verifier (TASK-020, TASK-021). **TASK-019 size ruling:** about 186 new lines in `db/maintain.py` plus the CLI and scrub changes stay as one task (not split). **Reviewer's statement-log test fixes** (compare exactly, collect `execute` lines as well, planted parameterised superuser query) go to the tester before CHK-M1-240. | User answers; lead rulings. |
| 2026-10-20 | **Amendment to #44, approved by the user (clock guard for `db maintain`).** `db maintain` still creates the missing partitions as before, but it REFUSES every DROP (exit 1, a clear message that names the reason and the clock value, no row data) when (a) no existing partition (the default partition excluded) contains `now`, or (b) the newest `job_created_at` found in `scan_result` is later than `now`. The refusal is atomic with the rest of the run's decisions: when it triggers, the run still creates the window and reports that drops were skipped. Rationale (reviewer, TASK-019): a clock two years fast would otherwise drop all 24 partitions, including data inside retention, irreversibly. Also folded into the same follow-up (reviewer nits): reject a `now` without a time zone with a clear error, and take `pg_advisory_lock` so two overlapping runs cannot both plan. This is TASK-019 revision 1, to be done after the TASK-020/TASK-021 chain tests so it does not delay them; it goes through test and review as usual and the M1 gate waits for it. | User answer on 2026-10-20; reviewer question on TASK-019. |

| 2026-10-20 | **Second amendment to #44, approved by the user: guard (c), the partition gap check.** The first guard only delays a large forward clock jump by one day: run 1 refuses drops but creates the window at the skewed time; on run 2 guard (a) passes on that new partition and guard (b) passes because no real row is later than a fast clock, so every in-retention partition is dropped (reviewer, confirming tester probe CHK-M1-186). New rule: `db maintain` also refuses every DROP when the existing non-default `scan_result` partitions, read before this run's creates, are not contiguous (any UTC month missing between the oldest and the newest). Accepted side effect: if `db maintain` does not run for more than about 3 months, a gap appears and drops stay refused until an operator checks (fails safe). Remaining residual risk (corrected after the reviewer's rev 2 pre-review): a forward skew of less than the window plus one month passes all three guards, because a skewed window that starts in the month right after the real one is contiguous with it; data can then be dropped up to about 5 months early (example: real date 2026-10-01, clock 2027-02-28, the second run drops 2025_08 to 2025_12, all inside retention). No partition-only check can tell such a window from a normal one; only a data-based check or the TS §8.4 clock-skew alert (M2) can. No further guard without a new owner decision. | 2026-10-22 | **User decision: no part of a DSN reaches stderr or the logs** (tester finding in TASK-046 TEST). When PostgreSQL refuses a well-formed TLS DSN, the `psycopg.pool` logger's own WARNING ("connection failed: connection to server at … port … failed: FATAL: password authentication failed for user …") reached the CLI's stderr through Python's last-resort handler, naming the database user, host and port (never the password). The existing `_ScrubFilter` in `db/pool.py` removes only the whole DSN string and the password. New rule: driver and pool log records about connection failures are logged by error type only, never their message text, in `serve` and the CLI alike; the CLI configures logging so no record reaches the last-resort handler. | 2026-10-23 | **TASK-051 revision 1 (lead ruling, TS §3.3 L218 "`online` if the last heartbeat is < 10 min old").** The GREATEST age base governs `stale`, `offline`, decommission and stale inventory; `online` additionally requires `last_heartbeat_at` itself to be under 10 minutes old. | 2026-10-23 | **Contract fixtures for lone surrogates are model-only (lead ruling, REQ-061 (3) scope).** A lone UTF-16 surrogate cannot be expressed in a portable JSON Schema pattern (RE2 has no surrogates), pydantic's JSON parser refuses `\ud800` as invalid JSON, and Go's `encoding/json` decodes it as U+FFFD, so the Go agent can never send one. The 4 lone-surrogate fixtures (manifest `schema_gap: "lone surrogate"`) must be refused by the model; the schema check expects them to pass the schema, and the M4 Go contract test asserts nothing for them. NUL, by contrast, is expressible and must be refused by the schema too, including recursively inside `os_info.raw` (TASK-048 r1 defect). M9 note: a Results body parsed with `json.loads` can carry a lone surrogate in `stdout`/`stderr`, which fails a later UTF-8 encode; M9 decides that rule with the NUL rule. | Lead, 2026-10-23. |
| 2026-10-23 | **TASK-048 r1 round 3: recursive `raw` schema uses a plain `$ref` (lead, on the reviewer's should-fix).** The round-2 node (embedded URN `$id` with `$dynamicRef`/`$dynamicAnchor`) does not resolve with `openapi.json` as the validation root, and Go Draft 2020-12 validators and OpenAPI tools (kin-openapi, oapi-codegen, openapi-typescript) handle `$dynamicRef` poorly, which risks REQ-061 for the M4 Go agent and G16. Replaced by a recursive Pydantic `TypeAliasType` emitted as `$defs/RawValue` (`components/schemas/RawValue` in the document) with `propertyNames` refusing NUL keys; no export post-processing, so `model_json_schema()` and `openapi.json` stay one schema. A pathologically deep `raw` may be refused, provided the answer is 400 `invalid_request`, never 500; the impl report states the depth. Carry-over: once TASK-074 exports the inventory route, CHK-M3-016 validates the `raw` fixtures against the component with the whole document as root. | Lead, 2026-10-23. |
| 2026-10-23 | **Old token after an unused rotation is capped at `last_rotation_at` + 30 days (user, on the reviewer's TASK-059 pre-review).** Gap: `rotate()` overwrites `token_expires_at` with the new token's expiry and `prev_token_expires_at` stays NULL until the new token is first used, so after a lost rotate response the old token was accepted with no expiry check for as long as the new token stays unused (up to about a year), contradicting TS §3.2 steps 7–8 and REQ-071 (7). No column holds the old expiry and no schema change is allowed. Rule: while the new token is unused, the previous token is accepted only while `now < last_rotation_at + 30 days`; after that it gets 401 `token_expired`. Rotation is allowed only in the last 30 days of a token's life (TS §3.2 L190, `issued_at + max(TTL − 30 days, TTL ÷ 2)`, TTL ≥ 60), so the cap is never earlier than the real expiry and at most 30 days later; the rest (≤ 30 days) is an accepted residual, documented in the README. The lost-response retry (`replace_unused_token`) keeps `last_rotation_at` (as built), so retries cannot extend the cap. Implemented in TASK-059 (token check) as revision 1; TASK-075 must keep `last_rotation_at` unchanged on the retry. The README (TASK-080) states the residual. | User, 2026-10-23. |
| 2026-10-23 | **NaN and ±Infinity fixtures are model-only (lead, same reasoning as lone surrogates).** JSON (RFC 8259) has no NaN or Infinity literal, and JSON Schema cannot express "not NaN": a `NaN` token is not valid JSON for strict parsers (Go `encoding/json` refuses it), and `1e400` is a valid JSON number that only overflows to infinity in a float64 parser. The model refuses them (PostgreSQL `jsonb` cannot store them) with 400; the schema check expects them to pass the schema, marked `schema_gap: "nan_inf"` in the manifest; the M4 Go contract test asserts nothing for them. TASK-059 r1 as built: an old token past the cap is checked in the expiry position of the #59 order (`token_expired`, no revoke, no alert, even with a wrong machine-id), and a missing `last_rotation_at` with a `prev_token_hash` set is refused. Both accepted. | Lead, 2026-10-23. |

| 2026-10-23 | **Peer address zone ids are dropped (lead, TASK-067 round-1 defect CHK-M3-204-scope).** A link-local IPv6 TCP peer is reported by CPython with its zone (`fe80::1%eth0`); `ipaddress` accepts it and PostgreSQL `inet` refuses it, so enrol and every bsa_ route answered 500. The zone names the manager's own interface, not the client, so `source_ip()` keeps the address and drops the zone (`fe80::1`), then converts IPv4-mapped peers as before; the address without its zone is what the CIDR check, the rate-limit and failed-check keys, `agent_ip_seen` and the request's `source_ip` see. | 2026-10-23 | **TASK-082 design (lead): option (B), a custom `APIRoute` subclass.** FastAPI 0.148 parses the JSON body in the route handler before solving any dependency, so no dependency can run first. On the two operations (`POST /api/v1/agent/enroll`, `POST /api/v1/auth/login`) a small `route_class` runs the per-IP limit (`allow_enrol` / `allow_login`, every attempt counted, 429 `rate_limited` with `Retry-After`) and only then delegates to FastAPI's own handler, unchanged. The limit moves out of `login_limit` and `enrolment_caller`; the `bse_` token check and every other check stay dependencies in their current order. | 2026-10-23 | **TASK-071 revision 1: SIGTERM stays bounded with the database down (lead, on CHK-M3-233-db).** Round 1: with PostgreSQL refusing connections, `SchedulerRunner.stop()` → `shutdown(wait=True)` waited for start-up runs whose every transaction waited the 5 s pool timeout, so SIGTERM took 46 s and the M2 G27 test (15 s bound; CHK-M2-257 states 10 s) went red. Rule: stop still waits for a run that is executing and never lets a run start or use the pool after the pool closes (REQ-080 (3), CHK-M3-233), but a run must not hold SIGTERM past the existing bound: on stop, the runner sets a stop flag that every run checks before each step and each transaction, so a run waiting on an unavailable pool gives up at its next check instead of opening the next transaction; `serve` exits 0 within the M2 bound with the database down and up. No production knob is added; the test launcher hook that shortens intervals stays in the test file only (checked: `runner.py` reads intervals from `system_schedule` and `serve.py` reads no environment knob for them), as the reviewer required, because a cadence or skip knob would break REQ-088. A stop that gives up a run is logged by job and type only. | Lead, 2026-10-23. |

A job's `pickup_by` comes back from the pull as `…T12:00:00.000000Z` (re-serialised by the contract type) while the stored payload holds `…T12:00:00Z`; both are the same instant in RFC 3339 and both match the contract, accepted. | Lead, 2026-10-23. |
| 2026-10-23 | **The inventory route documents its 422 (lead, option (a)).** `PUT /api/v1/agent/{id}/inventory` really answers 422 for a decoder refusal (TS §3.4 L240, #72 (b)), and the M4 Go agent needs it in the document. (a) **Rejecting R1** records the rejection on R1 only and leaves agent A and its current token untouched; a rejection decommissions and revokes the agent only while the agent is still `awaiting_approval` (the TS §3.2 L187 "new agent row" case). As built it revoked A's live token and silently undid R2's approval: a defect. (b) **Approving R1** re-links A to R1's token, exactly like approving a re-enrolment (#64 (d) "approving a later one replaces the token again"; (f) "approving either request makes the agent `enrolled` with that request's token"), with the same re-link fields and alert rules; 409 only for an already-decided request or an expired token (#64 (e)). | 2026-10-23 | **Agent path and query UUIDs: hyphenated form only; content coding case-insensitive (lead, on tester-e's TASK-073 notes 2 and 3).** Extending W1 ruling (3) (hyphenated RFC 9562 UUIDs on input) to the agent routes' path and query ids: `{id}`, `{job_id}` and `?job_id` accept only the 8-4-4-4-12 hex form (either case); the 32-hex, `{…}` and `urn:uuid:` forms that `uuid.UUID` also parses get 400 `invalid_request`. `Content-Encoding` is compared case-insensitively (RFC 9110 §8.4.1), so `GZIP` is gzip. For `{id}` (impl-manager's proposal, accepted): the token is still checked first, so a bad token is always 401 and counted; after a valid token, an `{id}` not in 8-4-4-4-12 form (either case; this includes plain garbage such as `not-a-uuid`) gets 400 `invalid_request`, and a well-formed id of another agent gets 403 `forbidden` as today; both sit where the 403 is today, uncounted and before the 30-per-minute limit (TS §6.1 "{id} must match the token's agent, otherwise 403" still holds for real ids; the tester updates CHK-M3-162's `not-a-uuid` case to 400). Both go into TASK-083 (already open on the agent routes), so TASK-073 and TASK-074 are not reopened; TASK-083's TEST re-runs their suites. Accepted as is: two simultaneous 409s past the threshold may write two flood entries (errs toward alerting); the pull validates stored payloads after commit (payloads come only from the scheduler). | Lead, 2026-10-23. |

Each `GET /api/v1/audit-log` item also returns `detail_text`, the exact string that was hashed (null when `detail` is null), alongside the parsed `detail`; the README's re-verification steps use `detail_text`. | 2026-10-23 | **Import cycle fixed in M3 (lead; found by tester-g at the gate, reproduced by the lead).** `import bitscan_manager.services.inventory` (or `.decode`, `.ingest`, `.upload`) on its own raises `ImportError` (partially initialised `decode`): `decode.py` imports `api.agent.schemas`, which runs `api/agent/__init__.py`, which imports `services.inventory.ingest`, which imports `decode` again. Hidden because the app, `serve`, the scheduler and every test load `api.agent` first. Any future entry point that imports the inventory services first would crash at start. Fix as **TASK-084** (impl-manager, a one-task follow-up): break the cycle at its smallest point, without moving the contract models out of `api/agent/schemas.py` (for example, the shared window constants move to a module both sides can import, or the agent package's `__init__` stops importing services at import time); plus a test that imports each `bitscan_manager` module in a fresh interpreter. Inconclusive: a timeout on both ports means the packets are not answered at all, neither accepted nor refused by WSL, which suggests the traffic doesn't reach WSL (Windows NAT and the Default Switch, or the Public Wi-Fi profile) rather than being stopped by the new rule. VM shut down again. Next step: re-test with a real listener on 8443 (needs a fresh session or the default permission mode); if it still times out, the next fix is a Windows-side forward from the Default Switch address (172.31.0.1:8443) to WSL, which needs the user as admin. | Lead, 2026-10-26. |
| 2026-10-26 | **VM → manager 8443 re-test with a real listener (lead, at the user's request): FAIL, conclusive.** A temporary `python3 -m http.server 8443 --bind 0.0.0.0` ran in WSL (Ubuntu-24.04, mirrored, eth1 192.168.1.81); `ss` showed it on 0.0.0.0:8443, and it answered from inside WSL. Results: (1) WSL → 192.168.1.81:8443: OK. (2) Windows → 127.0.0.1:8443 (raw TCP, and `curl.exe --noproxy`): OK. `Invoke-WebRequest` timed out only because it goes through the Windows proxy. (3) Windows → 192.168.1.81:8443: TCP connect fails (expected in mirrored mode without `hostAddressLoopback`). (4) bitscan-u2204 (now 172.31.3.227, default route 172.31.0.1) → 192.168.1.81:8443, → :8444 (control, no listener) and → 172.31.0.1:8443: all **time out** after 6 s; `curl` rc 28. The Hyper-V firewall rule `bitscan-manager` (TCP 8443 inbound, WSL) was present and enabled; the WSL Hyper-V firewall setting is Enabled, DefaultInboundAction Block, LoopbackEnabled True; Wi-Fi profile Public. **Conclusion:** the WSL listener and the Hyper-V rule are not the problem. VM traffic is NATed by Windows at 172.31.0.1 and lands on the Windows stack, which does not hand 192.168.1.81 to the mirrored WSL, the same way Windows' own connection to 192.168.1.81 fails. Only localhost reaches WSL. Options for the user (all need admin or a WSL restart; none applied): (a) a Windows portproxy from the Default Switch address to WSL localhost, `netsh interface portproxy add v4tov4 listenaddress=172.31.0.1 listenport=8443 connectaddress=127.0.0.1 connectport=8443`, plus a Windows Defender inbound rule for TCP 8443 on `vEthernet (Default Switch)`; the agent then uses `https://172.31.0.1:8443` (or the `mshome.net` host name), so the manager certificate must cover that name or IP, and 172.31.0.1 changes after a Windows reboot. (b) `hostAddressLoopback=true` under `[experimental]` in `.wslconfig` and restart WSL. Untested; it may fix Windows → 192.168.1.81 but may not cover NATed VM traffic. (c) Run the manager on Windows or in a third VM on the Default Switch. (d) An SSH reverse tunnel per VM (excluded by the 2026-10-26 pre-authorisation, (4)). VM shut down; listener and temp files removed. | Lead, 2026-10-26. |
| 2026-10-26 | **Re-check after the user added option (a) (lead): FAIL, two problems.** The portproxy `172.31.0.1:8443 → 127.0.0.1:8443` is present, iphlpsvc is running, and Windows listens on 172.31.0.1:8443 (PID 5596). (1) **Port clash:** in mirrored mode WSL shares the Windows port space, so with the portproxy holding 8443, WSL cannot bind 8443 on any address (127.0.0.1, 192.168.1.81 and 0.0.0.0 all give `EADDRINUSE`). The manager's default listen is `0.0.0.0:8443` (`config/startup.py`). Fix: point the portproxy at another WSL port, e.g. `connectport=18443`, and run the manager in WSL on `127.0.0.1:18443`; agents still use `https://172.31.0.1:8443`. (2) **VM → 172.31.0.1:8443 times out** (6 s, the same as the :8444 control), while Windows itself reaches 172.31.0.1:8443 (a connection reset, because nothing is behind the forward). So Windows Defender drops inbound traffic from the Default Switch. Without admin no rule named for 8443 or Bitscan shows in the active store, and port filters can't be listed. All three Defender profiles are DefaultInboundAction Block. Needed: an inbound Allow rule for TCP 8443 that covers the profile of `vEthernet (Default Switch)` (or all profiles), e.g. `New-NetFirewallRule -DisplayName 'Bitscan manager 8443 (VMs)' -Direction Inbound -Protocol TCP -LocalPort 8443 -LocalAddress 172.31.0.1 -RemoteAddress 172.31.0.0/20 -Action Allow -Profile Any`. VM shut down; temp files removed. | Lead, 2026-10-26. |

| 2026-10-26 | **TASK-135 blocking defect (tester-m6b, CHK-M6-112); r1 routed to impl-deploy (lead).** `release.yml` job `manager`, L216: `pip wheel --no-deps --no-build-isolation -w … manager`. pip resolves a bare `manager` in a non-editable install as a PyPI requirement, not the folder, and downloads the unrelated sdist `Manager-2.0.5` (seen in Ubuntu 24.04, pip 24.0, rc 2). So no release can build, and with `--no-build-isolation` a PyPI project named `manager` could run unhashed build hooks in the release job and plant a forged wheel. Fix: `./manager` with `--no-index`, plus a check of every pip call in the file. The lead checked the tree: the bare form appears only at L216 (`units.yml` uses `./manager`; `-e manager` in manager.yml and the README is an editable install, which pip resolves as a path). (3) **`trap` is treated like `eval`:** refused wherever it is the command word. (4) **Brace expansion in a `bash -c` script:** a command word with an unquoted brace expansion (`{a,b}`, `{x..y}`) is expanded (bounded) and each result is checked as the command word. If the expansion is over the bound or can't be resolved literally, it is refused. Plain `sh`/`dash` scripts are unchanged (no brace expansion there). | User, 2026-10-27; lead, 2026-10-28. |

| 2026-10-28 | **TASK-156 case (c) success (lead, pre-authorised; from TS §4.1 L515, L522 and L526, no deviation).** A successful import of a case (c) `uploaded/` file (any name other than a live or deleted `policy-<n>.bit`) is stored exactly like an upload. Before commit, `uploaded/.tmp-<id>-<sha256>` is written (O_EXCL\|O_NOFOLLOW, 0600, fsync). After commit, it is renamed to `uploaded/policy-<id>.bit`, and the foreign file is then removed, unlinked without following it. **Why not leave the foreign file:** L522 keeps uploads only as `policy-<id>.bit`, and L526 needs disk to match the catalog. A stale foreign copy would also roll the policy back at the next start-up once a newer version is uploaded, because its hash no longer equals `source_sha256`. A rejected or refused (c) file is left in place and reported by the integrity check (#103); a rejected one gets a `rejected` row at each start-up, which is accepted. **Helpers:** impl-manager-m7 exposes the upload staging helpers from `uploads.py` as a public API (TASK-155 revision 1, no behaviour change), and `folders.py` calls it rather than copying code. Both removals go through the #102 (c) self-event record when TASK-159 adds it. | Lead (pre-authorised), 2026-10-28. |

That includes a case (a) `policy-<n>.bit` whose content now names another policy; without it, that other policy would have no file and would be deleted at the next start-up (TS L526). The case (a) file is never unlinked, because it is its own policy's `source_file`, and the #103 check reports its hash difference. | 2026-10-28 | **TASK-157 rulings (lead, pre-authorised; from agreed answers, no spec deviation).** (1) **A failed rebuild follows #98 (j):** an internal error (database or disk) rolls the whole rebuild back. No `bit_import` rows are written, the task ends `failed` with `{error: <fixed text>}`, and one `bits.rebuild` entry (with its `policy_rebuilt` alert) records state failed in a final transaction. TASK-154 plan §8's "a failed rebuild's success rows say rejected" is superseded. (2) **Rebuild alerts follow #23 (agreed) and REQ-166 (6):** a rebuild raises one `policy_rebuilt`, one `policy_import_rejected` per rejected file, and a `policy.import`/`policy_imported` entry **only for a policy whose catalog rows the rebuild changed**. An unchanged file gets no `policy.import` entry; its outcome is in `task.result` and the `bits.rebuild` entry. "Changed" means any of: new, revived, renamed, moved or displaced; version bumped; any plugin added, updated or retired; any `changed_executes`; or the policy's catalog state (policy row including `policy_status`, its `policy_plugin` rows and its plugins) differs before and after the store, read in the same transaction under the lock. So every REQ-166 (1) corruption counts. Manual imports are unchanged: every one is audited and alerts (TS L530). This is **TASK-154 revision 2** (impl-manager-m7), with a re-test and re-review. (3)–(4) **TASK-156 revision 1** (impl-manager-m7b, a rename only, no behaviour change): `folders._read` becomes `read_file` and `folders._Relocate` becomes `Relocate`. The rebuild calls them, plus `StoreResult.remove_after_commit` after its commit, as import_one does. (5) The `start_task` / `run` split is accepted, and so is the per-file `PolicyManagedOnServer` (no row, listed as not imported). | Lead (pre-authorised), 2026-10-28. |

The writers import it without importing the watcher, so there's no cycle. The one-line hooks are small revisions by each file's owner, tested with that file: **TASK-155 r2** (impl-manager-m7): `UploadStage.after_commit` records `wrote(uploaded/policy-<id>.bit, sha)`, and `finish_interrupted` records each rename. **TASK-152 r1** (impl-manager-m7; delete.py's builder is no longer running): `remove_after_commit` records `removed(...)`. TASK-152 had the user's second approval, so it needs it again. **TASK-156 r2** (impl-manager-m7b): `Relocate.after_commit` and `_remove_leftover` record `removed(...)`. Recording happens just before the filesystem call, so a fast event isn't missed. TASK-160 then only wires `open()` before the start-up reconcile and `run()` after it. | 2026-10-30 | **TASK-221 (spawn) choices accepted (lead, pre-authorised).** (1) `Spawned` exposes `Wait()` over `os.Process` (the TASK-211 lint forbids exec types in job.go), and the pipes are `os.Pipe`, so a background child still writing can't have its pipe closed by `cmd.Wait`. (2) `RemoveTempDir` retries after a 0700 chmod of the directories (runner-owned), so a plugin's `chmod 000` can't block cleanup (REQ-240 (9)). It uses `unix.Chmod` because gosec G302 flags `os.Chmod` above 0600 even for directories, and `nolint` is banned. **reviewer-m10 confirms** this is a genuine false positive (a directory needs the execute bit), not a lint dodge, and that the chmod is limited to the plugin's own temp tree and never follows symlinks. | Lead (pre-authorised), 2026-10-30. |
| 2026-10-30 | **TASK-221 r1: confined temp-dir chmod (lead, pre-authorised, safer option).** reviewer-m10 found that `unix.Chmod` on a path follows a symlink that a parallel plugin of the same job swaps in between the walk and the chmod, so the 0700 chmod can land outside the temp tree. Bounded (same uid, no privilege gain), but it breaks "never follows a symlink". r1: open `os.OpenRoot(dir)`, walk `fs.WalkDir(root.FS(), ".")` and call `root.Chmod(name, 0o700)` (Go 1.27, confined to the root). Do not use `fchmodat(AT_SYMLINK_NOFOLLOW)`: it needs kernel 6.6 and Ubuntu 22.04 runs 5.15. If `os.Root` makes the gosec G302 workaround unnecessary, drop `unix.Chmod`; if G302 still fires, add a scoped, commented exclusion for spawn_linux.go in `.golangci.yml`, never a `nolint`. New tester check: swap a subdirectory for a symlink to a runner-owned directory outside the tree; that directory's mode is unchanged after RemoveTempDir, and the temp tree is still removed. | Lead (pre-authorised), 2026-10-30. |

| 2026-10-30 | **#140 reading: in-flight protection covers only an outstanding HTTP attempt, not the back-off (lead, pre-authorised, safer option; keeps TS §3.3 L226; on the morning list).** reviewer-m10 found that TASK-220's sender holds its batch in flight for the whole back-off during a manager outage (results.go:96-112). Ruling: #140's "until that post is answered" means until that HTTP attempt returns, and a 429, 5xx or network error is an answer. **TASK-220 r1:** release the batch (a new spool `Release`, next to Ack and Drop) after each failed attempt, before sleeping, and rebuild it with `Next` before the retry. A converted item then goes as its marker; a duplicate after a lost 5xx is absorbed by M9's first result wins. **TASK-212 change:** the `Release` method only, re-tested and re-reviewed as a TASK-212 r1 delta; its second approval is re-applied on a clean pass. **TASK-223 r1:** after any `Put` error, try to store a `spool_overflow` marker for every plugin of the job without a stored result; if even that fails, log it locally with the job id and count and set `last_error`, so no plugin is silently left unreported on the agent's side (the manager still shows it as not run). TASK-219's nit (a full spool turning a new `runner_unavailable` item into `spool_overflow`) is left as built and goes to the morning list. | Lead (pre-authorised), 2026-10-30. |

| 2026-11-01 | **TASK-224 r1: always kill the shell when a plugin is stopped (lead, pre-authorised; reviewer-m10c's blocking finding, confirmed in job.go:103-135).** `stop()` calls `s.proc.Kill()` only when `cg.Kill()` fails. A plugin whose shell moves itself out of `p-<n>` (TS §3.7 L284 expects plugins to try) lets `cgroup.kill` succeed while the shell survives. The timer or ctx branch then blocks on `<-waitCh` until that process exits, so the timeout isn't enforced, Run never returns, the after-job sweep never runs, and the runner refuses every later job `busy` until it restarts. Against TS §3.5 L249-L250 and "a plugin cannot outlive its job". r1: `stop()` always calls `cg.Kill()` and then `s.proc.Kill()`, ignoring an already-exited error. New delegated row as bitscan-runner: that plugin with `timeout_s` 2 gives `timed_out` true and exit 137, Run returns within about 10 s, and nothing survives; red on the current code. Fresh builder impl-agent-m10r, since the earlier builder's session ended. | Lead (pre-authorised), 2026-11-01. |
| 2026-11-01 | **TASK-221 r2: keep the main OS thread out of Spawn (lead, pre-authorised, option (a)).** tester-m10c found CHK-M10-128 part 2 red, delegated as bitscan-runner: after 20 spawns the main thread (tid == pid, m0) stays parked at nice 10 and I/O idle. Spawn's locked goroutine sometimes runs on m0, and when a locked goroutine exits there the Go runtime parks m0 instead of ending it, contradicting the code comment "the thread ends with this goroutine". No goroutine runs there again, so #136 (b) and REQ-239 (1)–(5), (7) hold, but `ps` and /proc stat show the runner at nice 10 against the plan row. Ruling: fix the product rather than accept or weaken the test. `cmd/bitscan-runner/main.go` gains `func init() { runtime.LockOSThread() }`, so the main goroutine holds m0 and Spawn's locked goroutines always get their own thread, which ends with them. The tester mirrors it with a TestMain in the delegated test binary. The check stays as written (every runner thread except a live plugin-starting one is at nice 0), plus a plant removing the init lock that must go red. impl-agent-m10r makes it TASK-221 r2. **Also recorded:** CHK-M4-223 is rewritten as CHK-M10-271 (9/9 plants red). G35 is green through a new manifest field `shared_files`, with both checks tightened and 5/5 plants red. | Lead (pre-authorised), 2026-11-01. |

| 2026-11-02 | **TASK-225 r1: clear `busy` before the connection is closed (lead, pre-authorised, safer option).** PR #18's second CI run is green everywhere (manager suite, G38 on both images, and on 24.04 the root-only step and the 37 delegated rows) except agent `go test` on 22.04: TestServeMalformed's subtests after the first got "refused busy". Cause (serve.go:76-79): the handler's defers run LIFO, so `conn.Close()` runs before `busy.Store(false)`. The client sees EOF and its next connection can arrive while `busy` is still true. The agent client retries `busy` for 30 s, so there is no functional loss, but the runner reports busy when it is idle, and a test-side retry would hide a stuck-busy defect. r1: reorder so `busy` is cleared once `handle` has returned (all job work and cleanup done) and before the connection closes, so the next connection after the close is always served. One-line product change, re-tested (TestServeMalformed repeated under -race in WSL and a plant restoring the old order) and re-reviewed, and TASK-225's second approval re-applied on a clean pass. | Lead (pre-authorised), 2026-11-02. |
| 2026-11-02 | **TASK-225 r1 scope (lead, pre-authorised, safer option).** (1) The builder's extension is confirmed: on the accepted path `handle` also closed the connection before `busy` was cleared, so it now wakes its reader with a past read deadline instead, and the connection is closed only after `busy` is cleared. (2) **The pre-existing fatal-path race is fixed in the same r1, not left:** after a failed sweep or temp cleanup (fatal, so cleanup can no longer be guaranteed), a connection `Accept` had already returned could win the busy CAS once `busy` was cleared, and run one more job before Serve returned the error. A job after a failed cleanup breaks "no plugin outlives its job" (TS §3.5 L250) and the fail-closed rule for executor errors. Fix: set an atomic fatal flag before `busy` is cleared, and check it in the accept loop (placed after the busy CAS; see the next row), so such a connection is closed without being served (and without a `busy` reply that the agent would retry). A test row and a plant are added. | Lead (pre-authorised), 2026-11-02. |
| 2026-11-02 | **TASK-225 r1: the fatal flag is checked after the busy CAS, not before (lead; confirms the builder's deviation, checked in serve.go:59-101).** In `once.Do`, `s.fatal` is written and then `failed` is stored; `busy.Store(false)` runs after that, in the deferred func. A CAS that wins has read that `busy=false`, so it happens after `failed` was set and must see it. A check before the CAS would leave a window (a fatal landing between the check and the CAS) in which one job runs after a failed sweep. A lost CAS while `failed` is set also sends no `busy` reply, so the agent doesn't retry into a dying runner. `s.fatal` is read only after `failed.Load()` is true, which removes the unsynchronised read. Accepted as built. | Lead, 2026-11-02. |

| 2026-11-08 | **TASK-237: the tokens step's future-row probe reads `user_token.created_at`, not `expires_at` (lead; plan correction within the approved REQs).** Raised by impl-manager-b. Plan TASK-242 §3 mapped `newest(user_token)` to `max(expires_at)`, which is always in the future while any live token exists (API tokens last up to 365 days, TS §6.2). With REQ-278's "rows dated more than 1 day after now" rule, the tokens step would then be skipped on every run and expired tokens never deleted, failing REQ-275 (1) and (4). `created_at` is manager-written (`DEFAULT now()`, TS §5.2), like the other probe columns (`scan.created_at`, `inventory_snapshot.collected_at`, `enrollment_request.decided_at`, `scan_job.finished_at`), so it detects a wrong clock as REQ-278 intends. The delete cutoff stays `expires_at < now − 90 days` (#150 (b)). Plan TASK-242 §3 is superseded on this one row. The tester checks both the skip (a token created 2 days ahead) and the delete (a token expired 91 days ago while a live token exists). | Lead, 2026-11-08. |
| 2026-11-08 | **TASK-237: `scan_chunk` takes no row lock (lead; plan correction forced by the TS §5.2 grants).** Raised by impl-manager-b. Plan TASK-242 §3's `SELECT … FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` needs UPDATE privilege on at least one column (PostgreSQL: row-locking clauses check `ACL_UPDATE`). TS §5.2 L1001–L1006 grants `bitscan_retention` only SELECT and DELETE on the nine retention tables, plus INSERT on `audit_log`, so the scans step would fail with "permission denied" on every run. Adding a grant would change the schema and TS §5.3 L1051's role model, so it is not an option. Corrected form: a plain `SELECT scan_id … ORDER BY created_at, scan_id LIMIT n`, then the per-scan `pg_try_advisory_xact_lock` on the M8 re-evaluation key (which needs no privilege) as the #150 (k) guard, then `DELETE … WHERE scan_id = ANY(…)`, which takes its own row locks. Cost: a delete waits on, rather than skips, an app transaction that holds a lock on a scan or job over 400 days old (a late intake or state write). Retention runs one instance at a time, so SKIP LOCKED was never needed between retention runs. A `lock_timeout` variant was declined as new behaviour. The tester adds a grant probe that the plan's locking form fails as `bitscan_retention` (permission denied), so a reintroduced `FOR UPDATE` turns red. | Lead, 2026-11-08. |

A second model of that name makes FastAPI rename both components with module paths, which changes the agent schemas in `openapi.json` and breaks REQ-273 (3). The fields are exactly as in the plan. TASK-239 (the web client) uses the new names. The reviewer checks that the agent components in `openapi.json` are unchanged from main. | Lead, 2026-11-08. |

Low risk: one approval, and a plant showing 84.99 fails and 85.00 passes. It strengthens a gate and lowers none. | Lead, 2026-11-08. |

For operator and admin each row may carry up to 64 KB of stdout and 16 KB of stderr (TS §4.5 caps), so one export can hold about 8 GB of rows in memory, and roughly three times that once it becomes text and bytes, on the single manager (TS §2.1). Any operator can reach this with a large enough scan, for example 20 plugins × 5,000 hosts. The spec sets no export size. Options: (a) keep as approved and record it as a known v1 limit; (b) **recommended:** a byte cap as well as the row cap, for example 400 `invalid_request` "Invalid request: the export is over N MB; narrow the filters" before any CSV byte, with N to be set by the user (for example 256 MB, measured on the stdout and stderr lengths in SQL first); (c) a lower row cap only when output columns are included (for example 10,000); (d) stream the CSV without building it in full. | 2026-11-09 | **PENDING USER: retention cannot keep up at fleet size without the #151 indexes; the M11 merge is held (lead).** tester-m11t (CHK-M11-387, interim) measured on data set A (10,000 hosts, 1.82 M `scan_job` rows), as `bitscan_retention` with `statement_timeout` 300 s. 100 single-host scans: 105–108 s. One fleet scan of 1,000 hosts (1,019 jobs): **over 300 s, cancelled**. 1,000 inventory jobs: **over 300 s, cancelled**. Cause, confirmed against TS §5.2: no index on `scan_job.retry_of` (a self-FK) nor on `scan_result_reeval.job_id` (FK ON DELETE CASCADE), so PostgreSQL seq-scans both tables for every deleted `scan_job` row, about 0.3–0.5 s per job, whatever the chunk size. Effects: a fleet scan of about 600+ hosts can never be deleted, and as the oldest eligible scan it stops every later scan's deletion; the inventory step fails every night, so inventory jobs are never deleted and the table keeps growing. The user's 2026-11-09 remedy (halve SCAN_CHUNK) cannot fix a per-job cost. The user accepted #151 (2026-11-08) as "slow but bounded"; this measurement shows it does not work at fleet size, so the decision is reopened. **Options for the user** (tester-m11t is measuring each on a throwaway database): (a) **recommended:** add the three #151 indexes (`scan_job(retry_of)`, `scan_job(scan_id)`, `scan_result_reeval(job_id)`). That amends the TS §5.2 DDL, which only the owner may do, and needs one migration reproducing it exactly; (b) a retention-only `statement_timeout`, plus jobs-first deletes in sub-chunks: it would complete, but a night's run could take hours at fleet size; (c) merge M11 with retention documented as not fleet-ready, and fix it in a follow-up before the first release. **Not changed and not merged:** the user's merge authorisation was "when the M11 gate and closing review pass", and the gate now carries an open finding only the user can resolve. Everything else continues: the gate tester and the closing review. | Lead, 2026-11-09 (pending the user). |
| 2026-11-09 | **Retention measurements for the pending decision above (tester-m11t, `work/reports/CHK-M11-387-test.md`; CHK-M11-387 FAIL as the v1 limit).** 10,000 hosts; A = weekly fleet scans (1.82 M `scan_job` rows), B = daily (5.3 M); real migrations; `bitscan_retention` through the real repository functions on a `serve`-built pool (`statement_timeout` 5 min). **As built:** 1,000 inventory jobs take 407 s (A) / about 497 s (B) uncapped and are cancelled at 300 s. One 1,000-host scan takes 481 s uncapped. One 10,000-host fleet scan takes about 80–85 min (estimated). A full nightly pass deletes nothing from the scans and inventory steps. EXPLAIN: each deleted `scan_job` row costs one seq scan of `scan_job` (`retry_of` FK, 238–385 ms) and one of `scan_result_reeval` (`job_id` cascade, 106–190 ms). **With the three #151 indexes on the throwaway database:** inventory chunk 0.25–1.19 s; 1,000-host scan 0.86–0.96 s; fleet scan 10.8–17.9 s; **full nightly pass 33.0 s (A) / 25.4 s (B), everything deleted**. The indexes build in 0.5–2.0 s and take 12–37 MB. **Options:** (a) as built: never completes; (a1) halve SCAN_CHUNK: same; (a2) JOB_CHUNK about 300: inventory only, 4.5–5.5 h a night, large scans still blocked; **(b) the #151 indexes: fits, 25–33 s a night (recommended)**; residual: a backlog chunk of 100 fleet scans would take about 20–27 min, so SCAN_CHUNK about 12 or less bounds it; (c) a retention-only lifted timeout: about 4.5–6.9 h a night, a bound of 1.5 h or more per fleet-scan chunk. Hardware caveat: a WSL2 laptop, ±40 %; a server might be about 2× faster, not enough without (b). Option (b) amends the TS §5.2 DDL (owner-only) and needs one migration reproducing it exactly, with grants unchanged. | Lead, 2026-11-09 (pending the user). |

| 2026-11-13 | **`bitscan-capread` reads the loaded AppArmor profiles (owner: "approve the capread wording").** Plugins need `/sys/kernel/security/apparmor/profiles`, which the kernel opens only for uid or gid 0: on the owner's host (k8s-worker01) the runner sandbox got EACCES, a dynamic user with `CAP_MAC_ADMIN` got EACCES, and a dynamic user in group root (no capabilities, then with the full helper unit settings) read it. TS §3.7 now gives the helper a third fixed request, `apparmor_profiles`, which reads that one file itself, and `SupplementaryGroups=root` in its unit; TS §8.1 records the accepted risk (a compromised helper could read group-root-readable files on that host). The unit copy in `deploy/systemd/` and `check_units_verbatim.py` line numbers change with the spec in the same docs PR, because the units are verbatim TS.
